On ImageNet $256\times256$ and $512\times512$, EiT consistently outperforms JiT (Just image Transformers) in generation quality. Specifically, our EiT achieves better FID than JiT: 1.61 vs. 1.85 at $256\times256$, and 1.73 vs. 1.99 at $512\times512$. Remarkably, EiT can be seamlessly applied to video pixel diffusion by simply extending the patch size to temporal dimension and outperforms video latent diffusion in FVD evaluated on Kinetics-600 (resolution 256), under comparable total network parameters. Code is available at this https URL


### [377] [Plausible Is Not Proven: Testing Failure Explanations in Monocular Depth Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34393)
*Zeeshan Ali, Md Rayyan, Anshuman Chakravorty, Omer Faraz, Anshi Bajaj, Amit Saini*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Failure analyses of monocular depth models often name a cause for each error, such as an occluding boundary, a thin object or a reflective surface, but such labels are rarely tested. We present the Vision Error Observatory (VEO), an audit that finds regions failing the $\delta_1$ criterion on sensor-measured pixels, labels each with a likely cause or abstains, and then subjects the labels to four tests: agreement with object annotations on data the label model never saw, agreement with blinded human raters, response to targeted image edits, and value as a training signal. We apply it to eight models on NYU Depth V2, DIODE and iBims-1, with labels also checked on Hypersim, and fix most decision rules before their results. The occluding-boundary label agrees with annotations (AUROC 0.85 on NYU, 0.81 on Hypersim, 0.69 on iBims-1), but a single depth-edge cue does as well, and most boundary failures of the strongest model vanish under a 2-pixel tolerance. The reflection label does not transfer to synthetic data, the textureless label is near chance against annotations, and only one of four edit tests, an exploratory one, supports its label. Evaluated against five blinded raters, VEO's labels matched the human consensus only at chance level (Cohen's $\kappa$ = 0.05). In addition, an increase in the weight on the diagnosed regions during fine-tuning led to an increase in the error on targeted pixels at every weight tested. The architecture-specific failure fingerprint claimed by an earlier version of this project also failed to replicate across the datasets. Plausible failure explanations, in short, need to be tested before they are trusted; we release the protocol, code, ratings and every result file.


### [378] [A Hard Background Pairing Transfers across Classifiers and Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34645)
*Karl Li*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: An image classifier names the object in an image, and its accuracy is the share of images named correctly. A classifier that names a car on a road may miss the same car pasted onto clouds. Composite benchmarks measure such failures by pasting objects cut from photographs onto backgrounds from other photographs. A pairing puts every object label on a different background label, such as truck on sea. The pairing on which some classifiers do worst, their hardest pairing, makes a strict test, but may be hard only for them or only on the photographs used to choose it. We show that the hardest pairing of some classifiers stays hard for others and on new photographs. We split 40 ImageNet classifiers into halves balanced by reported ImageNet accuracy. One half picks its hardest pairing of 8 object and 8 background labels from Common Objects in Context (COCO). The other half, held out of the choice, compares that pairing with random pairings of the same objects and backgrounds. For the 14 held-out classifiers with little net accuracy change after pasting objects back onto their own backgrounds, mean accuracy on composites from new photographs falls from 50.3% over random pairings to 43.7% on the hardest pairing, 6.6 percentage points lower. A benchmark using these composites can compare this pool's held-out classifiers on the fixed and random pairings.


### [379] [LiDAR Intensity Reconstruction with Limited Real Data: An Architecture Benchmark under Fixed-Epoch Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34724)
*Christopher M. Coovrey, Aniruddha Bora*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Physics-derived simulation can supplement scarce real-world LiDAR intensity measurements, but its effectiveness depends on the learning architecture and the amount of real training data available. We benchmark nine neural architectures for LiDAR intensity reconstruction from depth, semantic labels, incidence angle, and material reflectance, using real SemanticKITTI and synthetic VoxelScape range-image data. All models follow a fixed 20-epoch protocol; this controls the number of training passes, not the computational cost across architectures. We report mean absolute error (MAE), mean squared error (MSE), peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), structural similarity (SSIM), inference timing, and spectral-energy agreement. We also evaluate four selected architectures with fixed-size training mixtures containing 0\%, 1\%, 5\%, 10\%, 20\%, 50\%, or 100\% real frames. With real-only training, UNet-V2 achieves the best reported reconstruction metrics (MAE 0.234, MSE 0.140, PSNR 8.64 dB, and SSIM 0.647), followed by a denoising diffusion model (DDPM). In several mixtures containing 1--10\% real data, DDPM achieves lower error and higher image-quality metrics than the evaluated deterministic alternatives, whereas UNet-V2 leads with 100\% real data. Spectral analysis additionally reveals architecture-dependent discrepancies across frequency bands. These findings characterize reconstruction trade-offs under the stated data mixtures and fixed-epoch protocol, without establishing compute-matched superiority or isolating the diffusion objective from backbone differences.


### [380] [Was the Model Given the Right Action? Auditing and Repairing Action Interfaces in Navigation World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34739)
*Ajmain Istiak Apon, Md Mehedi Hasan, Sejuti Rahman*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: World models are evaluated by predicting future observations from actions. These evaluations assume that actions are prepared the same way as during training, but an unchecked mismatch can make a model appear less accurate than it is. We audit this assumption in Navigation World Models (NWM) and find three interacting differences between its training and rollout code. Correcting the inputs, without retraining, reduces mean visual prediction error by 42%, measured with DreamSim on the RECON navigation dataset. The correction also changes the ranking of NWM checkpoints in an exploratory analysis and removes or reduces two alternative models' measured advantages. Correcting only the normalization offset instead increases error, showing why interacting mismatches must be checked together. To test whether sensitivity to input preparation extends beyond NWM, we deliberately introduce encoding errors into two alternative models; both become less accurate. We introduce ActRosetta, a tool that checks whether action encoding matches training, identifies supported causes of disagreement, and repairs inputs when sufficient information is available. Repair helps little where predictions barely respond to actions, the tool's diagnosis has limited coverage, and improved control remains unproven. For any action-conditioned world model, the lesson is the same: before reporting rollout error, verify that evaluation encodes actions exactly as training did.


### [381] [Comparative Study of Deep Learning Architectures for Brain Tumour Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34781)
*Prisha Gupta, Pradeep Kumar Gupta*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Accurate and early classification of brain tumours from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is essential for treatment planning, yet many published deep learning studies are trained and tested on small, single-source datasets, reporting accuracies that may not hold outside these datasets. This study compares six deep learning architectures for four-class classification (healthy, meningioma, glioma and pituitary tumour): a baseline convolutional neural network (CNN), an attention-based CNN, ShuffleNet, and EfficientNet-B0, ResNet-50 and Xception augmented with the Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM). The models were trained on a corpus assembled from four publicly available MRI datasets. Because these datasets carry no patient identifiers, a leakage-aware pipeline was developed that removes exact duplicates using MD5 hashing (550 images) and near-duplicates using perceptual hashing (8,024 images), and assigns visually similar images to the same fold during 5-fold group cross-validation. A leave-one-dataset-out (LODO) test on a fully held-out dataset was used to measure cross-dataset generalisation. Under a conventional random split, ShuffleNet achieved 97.1% accuracy with 1.2M parameters. Under leakage-aware group K-fold cross-validation, ShuffleNetV2 (98.41%) and EfficientNet-B0 (98.23%) were statistically indistinguishable (paired t-test, p = 0.19), and both significantly outperformed the custom CNNs. On the held-out dataset, accuracy dropped for every model: EfficientNet-B0 generalised best (78.4%), while ShuffleNetV2 fell to 66.2%. Glioma-meningioma confusion was the dominant error mode, and Grad-CAM maps showed that predictions were driven by tumour regions. These results indicate that in-distribution accuracy alone overstates real-world performance and that leakage-aware and cross-dataset evaluation should be reported alongside it.


### [382] [Guidance as Variance Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34801)
*Ali Backour, Artem Lukoianov, Frank Permenter, Tommi Jaakkola, Justin Solomon, Vincent Sitzmann*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Guidance repels the prediction of a diffusion model away from a weaker reference prediction. It substantially improves sample quality, but its mechanism remains poorly understood. We first find that, at the scales used in practice, guidance does not improve the prediction of the posterior mean (the model's training target); it makes the prediction worse, while samples improve. Second, posterior-mean predictions have less variance than the data, and we find that guidance restores it. We introduce variance-matched guidance: a closed-form rule that picks the guidance scale by matching the variance of the guided predictions to that of the data. Without tuning, it recovers the hand-picked scales of eleven widely used models across image, video, audio, motion, and world generation; to our knowledge, no previous explanation of guidance predicts them. Applied per noise level, it yields a schedule without tuned hyperparameters that users prefer to hand-crafted ones on SD 3.5 Medium. Third, we offer an interpretation of the mechanism behind guidance: each model makes a random error in predicting the posterior mean, and guidance rescales the difference of these errors into a sample with the posterior's covariance, which improves sampling. This view also suggests two new hypotheses that our experiments support: guidance can work with negative scales, and guiding a model with its own checkpoint needs a larger scale than guiding it with an independently trained model.


### [383] [Focus Only on What's Important: Training-Free Focus Maps for Frozen Vision Transformers in Long-Video Object Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34863)
*Harsha Karipineni, Rajesh Reddy Chintha*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Vision transformers are strong but costly image encoders for video object segmentation. Training-free token merging cuts compute uniformly across the frame, including on tracked objects. We give a frozen DINOv3 ViT inside a trained Cutie head a training-free focus map: full resolution where the segmenter expects its objects and coarser merging elsewhere. Protecting only the previous frame's mask works on short clips but fails on long videos, where fast motion pushes targets outside the margin and into background merging, locking in loss. Our policy instead takes the union of velocity-shifted and block-matched masks, runs a camera-compensated surprise check, delegates uncertain regions to the compared merger, and falls back to it when all objects are lost. On long videos (LVOS V2 valid, pre-specified protocol, three ViT-S seeds), it beats ALGM 0.98 by +2.76 J&F (95% object CI [+1.22,+4.44]; video-cluster [+0.99,+4.52]) at 16% fewer counted encoder FLOPs (0.385 vs. 0.459), sitting 1.0 J&F point below the dense-encoder baseline; at ViT-B the gain over ALGM 0.95 is +3.89 [+2.03,+5.87]. On DAVIS 2017 val it stays within -0.19 J&F of the dense encoder at ViT-S (-0.12 at ViT-B). Controls qualify the result: protecting only the previous mask under ALGM matches or exceeds accuracy at higher FLOPs (0.486), so expectation and surprise mainly reduce counted encoder compute. Practically, lower counted encoder compute did not reduce startup-inclusive mean Cutie step time on a 12-video LVOS sample (3%-8% lower in steady state; an equally engineered ALGM is faster at ViT-S and ViT-B). Section 5.2 separately examines a Cutie permanent-memory schedule and its latency and memory trade-offs. Code: this https URL


### [384] [Stranger Things: When Objects Appear Without Their Typical Neighbors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34881)
*Siddhartha Gairola, Jiahao Xie, Anna Kukleva, Francesco Locatello, Bernt Schiele*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Visual context is a powerful cue for object recognition: scenes inform what, where, and at what scale objects are likely to appear. Yet over-reliance on context becomes a failure mode when models predict objects based on their typical neighbors rather than the objects' own visual features, which we call typicality bias. While extensively studied in the pre-Transformer era, it has often been assumed that architectural and scale improvements would naturally solve it, and a systematic diagnostic across the modern vision stack remains absent. We provide such a diagnostic framework. We measure typicality bias via normalized pointwise mutual information (NPMI) over object pairs, and introduce STRANGE-Bench (Synthetic Typicality-RANked Grounded Evaluation Benchmark): a photorealistic, layout-controlled benchmark spanning 17 sub-tiers from typical to never-co-occurring object pairs. Across five model families (specialist detectors, open-vocabulary detectors, self-supervised, vision-language, and multimodal large language models) and three recognition tasks (supervised detection, open-vocabulary detection, and classification), the bias is prevalent: gaps of 5-11 p.p. persist when objects appear outside their typical company, and do not consistently shrink with scale. To mitigate it, we turn to data augmentation: existing augmentations are designed for visual diversity, not for breaking co-occurrence shortcuts, and sample from the very distribution that produces typicality bias. We propose Anchor-Atypicality Sampling (AAS), a simple sampling rule for paste-based augmentations that uses the NPMI signal to tilt the training-time co-occurrence distribution toward atypical partners. AAS reduces the typicality gap by up to 1.6 p.p. on natural-image COCO splits and 23.0 p.p. on synthetic STRANGE-Bench across three model families, without sacrificing overall performance.


### [385] [What a Drop Reveals: Sim-to-Real Material Estimation for Deformable Objects from Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34894)
*Ziyue Peng, Yanxi Hu, Junyoung Kim, Andrew Chen, Jin Yang, Chandrajit L. Bajaj, Etienne Vouga, William T. Freeman, Qixing Huang, Brandon Y. Feng*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: How an object deforms when it hits the ground reveals what it is made of. From a single real video of a drop, we estimate a deformable object's density-normalized stiffness $E/\rho$, and its yield stress $\sigma_Y/\rho$ when the impact causes plastic deformation, with a feed-forward predictor trained only in simulation. Real videos carry no material labels, so such predictors are developed and validated on synthetic data alone. We show that synthetic validation ranks their geometric inputs in the wrong order. Material acts through 3D deformation, and tracklets lifted to 3D with estimated depth are indeed the most accurate input in simulation. However, on real drops they are the least accurate, and better depth does not repair them: the failure persists with simulator depth in training, LiDAR depth at test time, and exact depth on rendered replays of the real drops. Short 2D optical-flow tracklets do the opposite: they improve nothing in simulation, yet they transfer best. Material is therefore better read from what perception measures consistently in both domains than from what is most informative in simulation. Our predictor, PhysReader, reads the video and 2D tracklets and learns the lift to 3D in simulation by matching a teacher that sees exact 3D deformation. To measure transfer, we introduce PhysReader-Real: 16 everyday objects dropped five times each, with reference materials fit jointly to each object's drops and checked against tensile tests. On PhysReader-Real, PhysReader is closer to the reference materials than every other input and baseline. The one exception is a per-scene optimizer started from PhysReader's prediction, which ends slightly closer but takes 49 minutes per video instead of 12 seconds.


<div id='cs.SI'></div>

# cs.SI [[Back]](#toc)

### [386] [Anti-AI in the Wild: From Toxic Discourse to Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33254)
*Yage Zhang, Yukun Jiang, Ziqing Yang, Yihan Ma, Xinyue Shen, Yang Zhang*

Main category: cs.SI

Abstract: Online discussion of generative AI has become increasingly contentious, and communities devoted to supporting, opposing, or debating AI have emerged on social media. Some of these communities take anti-AI actions, such as deploying crawler tarpits and data-poisoning services to interfere with data collection and model training. Yet, the scope of these actions and their relationship to online discourse remain poorly understood. In this paper, we present the first measurement study of online conflict over generative AI, from toxic discourse to anti-AI actions. We collect 931,640 posts from 25 Reddit communities, label them with a large language model detector validated against human labels, identify 3,312 anti-AI actions, and trace the output of three deployed anti-AI tools. Our analysis reveals that 30.0% of posts in communities devoted to this conflict are toxic, compared to 4.4% in general AI communities. Anti-AI actions in these communities mainly target AI crawling or model training data, such as image cloaking and data poisoning. We further examine the implications of these anti-AI actions for AI training, by analyzing the text and code generated from deployed tools. Notably, 66.5% of the code samples generated by a data-poisoning service contain fabricated facts, misused APIs, or broken logic, yet 93.6% passes a public code-curation pipeline, close to the rate for real source code (95.0%). For crawler tarpit output, adding a period to the end of each paragraph increases the curation survival rate from 16.4% to 91.9%. We also find that crawler tarpit and data-poisoning output has already entered Common Crawl, a widely used public web archive, and that crawler tarpit output has also entered FineWeb, a released pretraining dataset. We hope our study can facilitate a better understanding of online conflict over generative AI and its implications for content moderation and AI training.


### [387] [Whose Side Are You On? A Framework and Benchmark for Assessing LLM Political Stances through Politicians Social Media Posts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34098)
*Mohamed Osman, Mucahid Kutlu, Tamer Elsayed, Saban Kardas*

Main category: cs.SI

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly participate in political discussions, making it important to understand which positions they support or oppose. We propose a framework for assessing LLMs' political stances through simulated social media interactions. The framework uses politicians' posts and multiple prompting methods to identify consistently expressed stances. Using this framework, we construct WorldPOLL, comprising 4,600 political posts from X/Twitter collected from approximately 200 politicians representing ruling and opposition blocs across 23 countries. Our quantitative analysis shows how support and opposition vary across models, countries, and political directions, while our qualitative analysis identifies the political views and policies models support or oppose. Together, these analyses reveal shared positions across models and differences across national contexts. We also examine whether models express consistent stances across prompting methods, investigate the reasons for disagreement, and assess the effects of model size and instruction tuning in stance inconsistency. WorldPOLL offers a practical way to measure LLM political tendencies, identify the views behind them, and assess their consistency in real-world political discussions.


### [388] [InterConRank: Joint Node Centrality in Interconnected Heterogeneous Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34763)
*Hao Ren, Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.SI

Abstract: Two nodes with identical connections within their own layer can differ in importance if their neighbors in another layer occupy different positions in the network. We introduce InterConRank, a centrality algorithm that computes a separate node ranking for each layer of an interconnected network. Directed dependencies specify which layers supply scores to each receiving layer. InterConRank normalizes each type of link separately and updates all layers together, so a score can cross into a layer in one iteration and propagate along its internal links in the next. We prove convergence of this joint update and evaluate the resulting rankings on four scholarly networks. On all four networks, InterConRank gives the highest rank correlations with both h-index and total citations for nodes in the collaboration layer.


<div id='cs.NI'></div>

# cs.NI [[Back]](#toc)

### [389] [Spectrum Adaptation of AI-Native Autoencoder Communications with Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34942)
*Yalin E. Sagduyu, Tugba Erpek*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: End-to-end physical-layer learning replaces separate modulation, coding, and detection operations with an autoencoder (AE) that consists of a jointly trained encoder-decoder pair at the transmitter and the receiver. This paper studies AI-native communications with AEs and quantifies the sensitivity to training-test mismatch in channel models. Models trained only on an AWGN channel achieve low bit error rates (BERs) under matched conditions but degrade severely on Rayleigh fading with practical front-end impairments. To reduce the time and energy required to adapt to new channel conditions, we propose a low-latency transfer-learning procedure that starts from an AWGN-pretrained system and fine-tunes only a small subset of transmitter and receiver layers while freezing the remainder. We further formulate layer selection as a budget-constrained optimization problem that identifies the most informative parameters to update during adaptation. This preserves the learned signal geometry and the deep decision structure while adapting lightweight front ends to fading- and impairment-induced mixing. For AE communications, we show that partial updates remove the mismatch-induced error floor and approach the BER of fully retrained models. We provide a layer-wise study and quantify resource-performance trade-offs, showing that modest adaptation substantially reduces BER while cutting training time, multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations, energy consumption, and the fraction of updated parameters for resource-efficient implementation.


### [390] [Feasibility-Aware Deep Reinforcement Learning for O-CU and O-DU Mapping in 6G O-RAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34516)
*Mohammad Asif Habibi, Xavier Costa-Perez, Hans D. Schotten*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: This paper studies the mapping of the virtual network function components (VNFCs) of the O-RAN central unit (O-CU) and O-RAN distributed unit (O-DU) onto virtual machines (VMs) in sixth-generation (6G) open radio access networks (O-RAN). The problem is formulated as a feasibility-aware sequential decision process that maximizes the number of placed VNFCs and, secondarily, favors fewer active VMs and lower residual resource wastage. Although conventional optimization techniques can solve this problem, their computational cost becomes prohibitive for larger instances or when optimization must be repeated. To address this limitation, we explore two neural reinforcement learning (RL)-based methods: a deep $Q$-network (DQN) and a double deep $Q$-network (Double DQN). Whereas DQN uses the same target network to both select and evaluate the bootstrap action, Double DQN decouples these two operations to mitigate maximization-induced overestimation. We compare both methods against six baselines: random feasible placement, first-fit, first-fit decreasing, best-fit, a resource-wastage-aware heuristic, and a time-bounded mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) solver. We conduct a controlled experimental evaluation across four problem sizes with $30$ independent instances each, together with capacity-stress, distribution-shift, and reward-ablation experiments. For the $16 \times 8$ VNFC-to-VM scenario, DQN and Double DQN achieve mean placed-VNFC fractions of $0.990$ and $0.996$, respectively. However, first-fit decreasing and the resource-wastage-aware heuristic match this placement quality with lower efficiency cost and lower decision time, whereas the MILP attains the lowest efficiency cost at a much higher runtime. The results clarify the strengths and limitations of neural placement for this static problem, without establishing superiority over conventional methods.


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [391] [Beyond the Efficiency-Efficacy Dilemma: A Critical Analysis of Transferability in Adversarial Malware Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33243)
*Veysel Alevcan, Mohammad Furqan Ali, Teresa Sousa, Luís Miguel Campos, Christoforos Ntantogian*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Machine Learning (ML) models are already being deployed at scale for malware detection. Therefore there is a pressing need to rigorously study adversarial robustness. To that end, we introduce the Adversarial AI Engine (AAIE): a platform for automated security stress-testing. We performed transfer attacks, via EMBER2018, against a Random Forest target. Counterintuitively, we found single-step FGSM to have the highest attack success rate (ASR) of 52.9% compared to iterative PGD with 45.2% (p < 0.002). Compared to iterative attacks which tend to overfit surrogate networks creating brittle perturbations, FGSM created more transferable examples. We benchmarked our attacks using ASR, transferability score, evasion rate, and resilience index. Through these metrics, proposed in concordance with emerging standards in AI governance, we expose shortcomings as our transferability scores peak at ~0.8 with constrained perturbations. We highlight the practical security implications of these findings and demonstrate AAIE for automated adversarial auditing.


### [392] [Onnx2TEE: Automated Graph-Aware Model Partitioning for TEE-based Edge AI Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33262)
*Daniela M. Vela Hernandez, Samuel Hildebrand, Sean Oesch, Brian Weber, Shayan Monadjemi*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: AI models are increasingly being deployed at the edge. Since these models often require significant research and development to produce, are often deployed in potentially hostile environments, and downstream systems often rely on accurate data; protecting them from theft or manipulation is worthy of serious consideration. Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs), which provide hardware-enforced separation of software execution, can protect model weights but impose strict resource constraints, making full-model execution impractical for large neural networks. We present Onnx2TEE, an automated, model-independent pipeline that partitions ONNX models across rich, trusted execution environments for non-sequential feed-forward networks using graph-aware segmentation to preserve computational dependencies while minimizing boundary communication and reducing TEE-induced inference latency. Evaluation on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin using an Arm TrustZone TEE demonstrates that model parameters can be highly concentrated in individual segments, supporting selective protection of parameter-dense model components. The evaluation further shows that the generated trusted app's memory requirements depend on both computation and intermediate tensor sizes.


### [393] [Poisoning Self-Evolving Security Policies in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33385)
*Aymen Noor, Honglu Jiang*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Self-evolving defenses improve LLM-agent security by learning reusable policies from observed attacks. However, we demonstrate that this learning process itself can become an attack surface. Using SED (Self-Evolving Defense) in the AgentDojo benchmark, we construct a single prompt-injection episode that causes the defense to learn an over-broad policy and subsequently refuse legitimate requests. Across 10 Nemotron runs, every poisoned run over-refuses at least one legitimate task, while unrelated-task performance remains unchanged and the original prompt-injection defense remains effective. The attack also transfers to gpt-oss-120b; however, its poisoning mechanism depends on a successful initial prompt injection and therefore fails to activate when the target agent resists the underlying injection. In contrast, integrity-oriented attacks targeting SED's judge, policy synthesis, retrieval, and organization mechanisms fail in our evaluated settings, identifying over-refusal as the primary exploitable failure mode observed in our experiments. We further identify self-poisoning from false-positive harm judgments without an attacker. Finally, contrastive policy synthesis and an automatic broadness-check gate substantially reduce, but do not eliminate over-refusal, without compromising security in our evaluated settings.


### [394] [SecID: An LLM-Guided Framework for Threat-Aware Secondary Asset Identification with Quantitative RTL Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33453)
*Md Ajoad Hasan, Dipayan Saha, Upoma Das, Sujan Kumar Saha, Mark Tehranipoor, Farimah Farahmandi*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: The growing complexity of modern hardware designs is making security assurance increasingly difficult. One of the foundational steps in pre-silicon security verification is identifying security assets. While primary assets are often evident from design functionality, the internal signals that can facilitate their compromise are much less apparent. Complex data- and control-flow dependencies create many such internal security-critical locations, whose exploitation depends on the threat-specific attack mechanism---these are the secondary assets. To precisely identify these, we propose SecID, a multi-agent framework that combines deterministic RTL analysis with bounded dynamic evaluation and LLM reasoning to identify threat-specific secondary assets. Each asset decision is supported by functional influence, threat features, and explicit attack mechanisms. We demonstrate SecID on seven open-source SoC and IP designs, including openMSP430, NEORV32, and CV32E40P. The experimental results are corroborated through comparison with prior work, fault simulation, and formal analysis, and further evaluated through pipeline- and final-stage ablation, threshold-sensitivity studies, and final-stage repeatability analysis. Together, these evaluations show that SecID can identify highly threat-relevant secondary assets with respect to a given primary asset, providing a scalable path toward hardware-security verification.


### [395] [Benchmarking Open-Ended Vulnerability Discovery in Windows Kernel Binaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33486)
*Jongseong Kim, Hwiwon Lee, Dongjun Kim, Lingming Zhang*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Frontier models are increasingly used to discover real-world security vulnerabilities, making it critical to systematically evaluate their cybersecurity capabilities. However, existing security benchmarks primarily focus on known vulnerabilities in open-source projects, largely overlooking closed-source binaries, which underpin much of the software industry. In this paper, we ask: how effective are frontier models at uncovering deep vulnerabilities in production binaries? To answer this question, we present DeepBinary, the first comprehensive benchmark for open-ended vulnerability discovery in Windows kernel binaries. Given a target binary, an agent analyzes it using a disassembler and kernel debugger in network-isolated VMs and reports each vulnerability with a crashing proof of concept (PoC). Unlike existing benchmarks, DeepBinary replays every report on fresh VMs and employs an LLM-based judge to assess its validity, uniqueness, and impact (estimated by bug bounty values). Beyond known vulnerabilities, DeepBinary also enables the discovery and validation of previously unknown (0-day) vulnerabilities. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of representative open- and closed-weight models (including GPT-6 Sol, Claude Opus 5, and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash) across diverse coding agents (including Codex and Claude Code) on 12 target binaries. In total, they discover over 200 vulnerabilities with an estimated aggregate bounty of $661,000, including 146 distinct previously unknown vulnerabilities across all experiments. Our results further reveal substantial differences in models' ability to analyze binaries, conduct experiments, and sustain vulnerability discovery over long horizons. Overall, DeepBinary makes long-horizon, open-ended vulnerability discovery in complex binaries measurable, revealing fundamental differences in how frontier models analyze and explore such systems.


### [396] [PRECEPT: Security Self-Improvement of Coding Agents via Evolving Principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33519)
*Keyan Guo, Rupam Patir, Haipeng Cai, Hongxin Hu*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: AI coding agents increasingly write and submit code with limited human review, and much of it passes functional tests while remaining insecure. We show that these failures persist. Across 14 task sequences, Codex, Claude Code, and Qwen Code fixed a vulnerability 85.7% of the time when shown the failing security test, then reintroduced the same vulnerability class in a new session 26.7% of the time, about as often as agents that had never seen it (27.5%). The capability is present; the retention is not. Neither recording the repair in the agent's persistent context nor supplying published secure coding principles closed the gap: the recorded fix stayed tied to the family of the task it came from, and the principles, written for human developers, remained too general to apply to the code at hand. The knowledge exists; it is not activated. We call the missing ability security self-improvement: an agent learns from its own security failures so that each lesson persists across sessions, applies to new code, and does not break what works. We present PRECEPT, which equips coding agents with this ability through an evolving library that starts from authoritative secure coding principles and revises them against the agent's own failures, so that each principle comes to fire on the code where that agent actually goes wrong. A revision is kept only if fresh executions show it makes code more secure while keeping functional success within a small tolerance of the agent without the library. Improvement continues across evolution steps rather than saturating after the first. On task families never seen during evolution, PRECEPT's weakest agent reaches 97.1% secure working code, above the strongest unaided agent at 81.9% and the strongest baseline at 87.7%.


### [397] [The Detour Is the Danger: Preserving Security During LLM Agent Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33605)
*Boyang Ma, Jinku Li, Hao Wu, Sheng Gao, Man Zhou, Minghui Xu, Yue Zhang*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on external tools and reusable Skills to execute complex workflows, autonomously replanning when operations fail. However, such recovery may preserve task functionality while silently discarding the security protections required by the original workflow. In this paper, we investigate workflow security downgrade, a failure-induced security threat in which agents execute sensitive operations without satisfying their original protection requirements. Unlike prompt injection, this threat exploits autonomous recovery decisions triggered by execution failures, without modifying instructions or tool definitions. We systematically evaluate this behavior through fault injection in an executable MCP/Skill environment spanning seven LLMs, twelve task templates, and three security-sensitive domains. To address this threat, we propose ARGUS, an obligation-preserving recovery framework that combines resource-bound security evidence, isolated recovery-plan validation, and execution-time enforcement. In a separate 864-run evaluation, ARGUS prevents all observed protection-loss events under enforcement, while recovery assistance yields model-dependent improvements in protected task completion over blocking alone. Our findings demonstrate that secure agent recovery requires preserving security obligations across workflow changes, rather than merely restoring task functionality.


### [398] [FlashProve-Next: Verifiable Inference for Production LLM APIs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33741)
*Liang Song*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Clients of closed-weight LLM APIs cannot verify whether a provider ran the advertised model, precision, hidden prompt, and reasoning budget. Existing approaches either trust hardware or third parties, or re-prove every layer of an integer surrogate at high cost while diverging from native floating-point serving. FlashProve-Next produces verifiable, zero-knowledge-masked proofs for individual LLM API responses without modifying native serving (bf16/fp8/mxfp4, random sampling, FlashAttention, CUDA graphs, batching, prefix caching) and without disclosing weights, hidden prompts, hidden reasoning, or activations (dense profiles fix coarse geometry; cross-architecture hiding is deferred). A non-depleting Reed-Solomon weight commitment model_id opens inside proofs via hidden column openings, while local per-operator rounding contracts remove integer surrogates and the refusal-bit leak. Cost-weighted spot-checking supports non-interactive public verification (NI-315, $\varepsilon \le 2^{-40}$, $Q \le 2^{64}$) and external-seed C_pre mode (ExtSeed-153, $\varepsilon \le 2^{-20}$, $Q_{\rm samp} \le 2^{30}$). Twenty-eight Lean 4 modules (696 declarations, zero sorry, standard axioms only) mechanize round-by-round soundness, bucketed sampling, $\mathbb{F}_{p^3}$ constrained-mask binding, causal masking, six IR gadgets, and executable FPN2 verifiers. Five-model Blackwell calibration gives zero false rejections across 1.85M GEMM/MoE blocks and 1.96M non-GEMM rows. Our zero-Phase-2 folded backend (folded ZK simulator open) proves Qwen3-0.6B ExtSeed-153 (1.47B cells, 8 MiB) in 450.2 s GPU (10.76 s CPU) at unique-decoding $Q=128$, passes a 14/14 cheating matrix, and scales to 36-layer Qwen3-4B (dev $4+2$) and live vLLM Qwen3-30B-A3B-FP8 Relation 6 (1.49 s prove, 23.4 ms Rust verify, public weight tiles). Rust fpn-verify checks 8 MiB FPN2 Relation 2 proofs in 10.1 ms on CPU.


### [399] [Agentic-Level Adversarial Purification: Revisiting Diffusion Purifiers on Native-Resolution Agentic Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34181)
*Yan Lin, Zhongliang Guo, Jingyu Lin, Daizong Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Vision-language agents act on native-resolution renders of multi-element pages, where one perturbed photo or a few altered text strokes can redirect the decision. Any content provider can craft such a perturbation on the open model behind the agent, so a plug-in purifier is the natural first line of defense. Yet diffusion purifiers were designed and evaluated at the fixed resolution of the diffusion model on classifier or natural-image benchmarks, and defense evaluations for agents test JPEG, blur and noise but no diffusion purifier. We present the first study of adversarial purification at the agentic level, for an agent built on Qwen3-VL-8B: purifiers run at native resolution on multi-element inputs, face grey-box attackers who know the agent's model but not the defense, and are compared at matched clean cost. We show that treating agentic inputs as classifier inputs misleads both evaluation and design. Resizing the input to the resolution of the diffusion model, as existing purifiers do, raises the TextVQA clean cost of the Divide-and-Conquer purifier (DC) from 4.0 to 35.7 points. On web-shop pages, DC keeps 85.0% grey-box robust accuracy against web-hardened perturbations, where cheap input transformations at matched clean cost keep at most 40.0%, and this robustness comes from the global noise level: input-estimated masks and noise levels add none. These findings hold on a disjoint held-out set and on a second VLM and yield three requirements for defenses, we release the protocol.


### [400] [Latent-in-the-Middle: On the Security of Cross-Model Latent Communication in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34278)
*Manit Baser, Vyoman Jain, Rachel Yeo Hui Min, Xianglin Yang, Meenatchi Sundaram Muthu Selva Annamalai, Dinil Mon Divakaran*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: As large language models increasingly collaborate in heterogeneous multi-agent systems, latent communication has emerged as an efficient alternative to exchanging natural-language messages. By sharing internal representations, agents can avoid repeatedly processing the same context, reducing communication overhead and prefill computation. One such mechanism is cross-model Key-Value (KV) cache transfer, which uses a learned mapper to translate a source model's KV cache into one a target model can use. However, this handoff means the target model consumes context it did not encode itself, thus calling into question whether its safety alignment still holds. In this work, we investigate the security implications of latent handoffs by introducing Chasm, a framework characterizing cross-model handoff attacks across three new attack surfaces: the source model, mapper, and handoff mechanism itself. Our experiments reveal that latent handoffs substantially undermine safety alignment and prompt-injection defenses. Notably, a target model that follows only 0.4% of benchmark prompt injections natively, follows up to 44.2% when transferred through a mapper from a source model. On the other hand, for jailbreak attacks, transfer alone raises attack success by up to 24.6 pp within a model family and zero-shot attacks by up to 69.2 pp, whereas white-box optimization through the mapper boosts the attack success rate to 92.5%, against 37.5% for ordinary GCG on the same target. To address these vulnerabilities, we propose a lightweight target-side cache guard that corrects transferred caches to restore the target model's refusal, considerably mitigating these attacks.


### [401] [Real Package, Wrong Controller: Static Auditing of Vendor--Protocol Mismatches in LLM-Generated PLC Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33227)
*S M Zia Ur Rashid, Sazidul Islam Hira, Suman Rath*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Language models can generate Python that drives industrial protocols on programmable logic controllers, yet existing code-grounding checks ask whether an imported package or symbol exists rather than whether it suits the declared industrial target. We define \emph{metadata-conditioned, import-level vendor--protocol incompatibility} and build \textsc{Proteus-OT}, a label-free static verifier over a version-pinned graph of documented bindings. Across 883 generated scripts, 180 carry an import-level mismatch against the declared target (20.4\%); restricting to single-vendor targets gives 16.3\%, and additionally requiring the offending library to reach a protocol communication call gives 14.2\%, which we read as conditional on the corpus metadata rather than on verified intent. None of the 28 adjudicable human control files carries one, a sample too small to estimate a prevalence. Within the balanced subset, 96.6\% of mismatching scripts import packages and call methods that exist, so an existence baseline does not represent them, and every positive ships with an inspectable witness. We also audit machine-versus-human classification on the same corpus, where character $n$-grams reach macro-F1 1.000 under task, model and vendor-disjoint partitions and survive comment removal, an AST round trip and identifier anonymisation. The trailing newline present in 95.2\% of human files and in none of the generated ones separates the classes on its own. Vendor--protocol consistency is a useful static audit target, whereas provenance classification on repository-labelled corpora is not authorship detection.


### [402] [Node Differential Privacy via Node-Stable Graph Projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33672)
*Ishaq Aden-Ali, Adam Sealfon, Adam Smith, Thomas Steinke, Jonathan Ullman*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Node-differentially private algorithms enable learning on network-structured datasets in which records reflect interactions among individuals. They protect the privacy of each individual's information, including all their associated records. A key challenge in the design of such algorithms is to exploit the structure of realistic graphs---where most nodes have low degree---to achieve high accuracy, without compromising on the worst-case privacy guarantee. In this work we give two new tools for designing accurate, computationally efficient node-differentially private algorithms. First, we give a nearly linear time, randomized, node-stable graph projection: the projection leaves graphs of maximum degree $D$ unchanged, maps arbitrary graphs to subgraphs of maximum degree $\hat D \approx D$, and ensures that changing one node of the input graph has only a small effect on the distribution of the edges selected among the remaining vertices. Unlike prior approaches, which abstain when the input violates the degree bound, our projection acts as degree clipping: even when $D$ is set well below the true maximum degree (trading truncation bias at high-degree outliers for lower privacy noise), it retains at least as many edges as a maximal $D$-bounded subgraph. Second, for graphs with bounded edge weights, we give a deterministic nearly linear time Lipschitz extension for the total edge weight that agrees with the original sum on graphs of maximum degree $D$ yet is $D$-Lipschitz on all graphs. Finally, we show how to apply these methods to design node-private algorithms for high-dimensional vector summation and gradient descent on datasets where records are associated with graph edges, such as email threads.


### [403] [From Models to Services: Systematizing Security Assurance for Model-as-a-Service](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34623)
*Jianming Zhou, Yang Luo, Guangpu Chen, Zhaoyu Chen, Yifeng Gao, Qingni Shen, Zhonghai Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Large language models are mainly delivered as cloud services, in which the assets of different principals are coupled within a single service pipeline, while the related security research is scattered across several fields with differing threat models. This paper systematizes this research from a service perspective. We decompose Model-as-a-Service (MaaS) into a five-layer stack and five trust boundaries, define six adversary classes and six security goals, use them to code 120 studies, and evaluate 50 defenses under a unified set of criteria. The results show that 44.2% of the studies concentrate on behavioral integrity, while service integrity, availability and cost, and accountability together account for only 13.3%. Defenses are evaluated on production services at less than half the rate of attacks, and none of the cryptographic or hardware mechanisms that protect against the provider can be adopted unilaterally by customers. Finally, we propose a research agenda by comparison with current standards and regulations.


### [404] [Make the Adversary Spend More: Stateful Deception as a Cost-Exchange Primitive for AI-Era Cyber Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34770)
*Alessandro Cantelli-Forti, Isabella Marasco, Hosam Alamleh, Giada Boschi, Michele Colajanni*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are transforming the economics of intrusion by reducing the attacker's cost to scan and adapt, while tempting defenders into unbounded compute. We argue that cyber deception should be judged by the cost exchange it imposes, not by realism or detection, and that the primitive making that exchange favorable is adversary-specific persistent state. Rather than a honeypot that resets or merges sessions, each recognized adversary re-enters the world it believes it has compromised, kept coherent with its own past actions and isolated from every other adversary, while the language model acts only as a constrained renderer of explicit state, never as its store. We formalize the trade-off as a Deception Exchange Rate (DER), relating mission-weighted adversary effort burned to defender resources spent, and instantiate it in an SSH honeypot pairing Cowrie with a local LLM. In this paper, we focus on the defender-side evaluation of that exchange, while specifying, but not yet measuring, per-adversary state and the adversary side of the DER. Across five open-weight models, deterministic-layer and prompt optimizations cut per-session defender cost by around 63% against a naive all-LLM decoy.


### [405] [A Survey of Security, Privacy, and Ethics of Large Language Models in Telecom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34868)
*Joy Bose, Srinivas Adyapak*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into telecommunications for customer service, network analytics, and operational support. While offering efficiency and innovation, their deployment raises critical privacy, security, and ethical challenges due to the uniquely sensitive and longitudinal nature of telecom data. This paper presents a survey of LLM risks and safeguards in telecom context. Our survey introduces a telecom-specific taxonomy linking privacy, security, and ethics with use cases and outlines open research challenges such as longitudinal data privacy, cross-border compliance, and scalable guardrails.


### [406] [Model Weights, Airlocks and Sandboxes: A Call for Technical Validation of Re-identification Tests under the EDPB Anonymisation Guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34859)
*Rickard Brännvall, Henrik Forsgren, Erik Ylipää, Aslak Felin, Michael Popoff, Susanne Stenberg*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: The Guidelines 02/2026 on anonymisation of the European Data Protection Board (EDPB), published for consultation in July 2026, assess anonymity relative to the entities that may access data. They bring AI models into scope and call for testing, but do not say how a re-identification test should be designed or validated, or how far its result counts as evidence. Building on our reply to the consultation, we describe a framework based on a secure processing environment whose outputs are reviewed before release (an airlock), and distinguish forms of release and three types of evidence: formal, empirical and indirect. We raise seven open questions, each with a scenario, and examine their technical side in three settings: the release of model weights (the trained parameters of a machine learning model), review at an airlock, and regulatory sandboxes, in which an authority supervises development and testing. In each setting the assessment depends on how far a test result can be relied on, since a failed re-identification attempt may mean only that the test was too weak. Drawing on measurement practice in other regulated fields, we call for re-identification tests to be validated as measurement methods, with stated detection limit, bias and uncertainty.


<div id='astro-ph.CO'></div>

# astro-ph.CO [[Back]](#toc)

### [407] [Scaling and Cross-Simulation Generalization of Graph Neural Networks for Cosmic-Web Classification in CAMELS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34332)
*Kalash Thakkar, Unnati Shah*

Main category: astro-ph.CO

Abstract: Graph-based methods can classify galaxies by their dark-matter cosmic-web environments, but reliability beyond development simulations remains uncertain. Galaxy-formation models alter tracer populations and spatial distributions, potentially shifting graph features. We test how graph neural networks scale with independent training realizations and transfer across hydrodynamical simulations. Using the $z=0$ CAMELS IllustrisTNG, SIMBA and Astrid suites, we label galaxies with $M_\star>10^9\,M_\odot$ as void, sheet, filament or knot from the smoothed cold-dark-matter density field. We represent their spatial distributions as periodic Delaunay graphs with ten geometric descriptors. We compare a multilayer perceptron (MLP) and five graph neural networks (GNNs), including a local graph Transformer, under realization-grouped evaluation. Learning curves increase training data from 10 to 100 realizations, with validation and test boxes fixed. We test every source-to-target suite pairing on 100 unseen realizations per target suite. On this external holdout, the Transformer, GAT+ and EdgeConv reach mean per-realization macro-averaged F1 scores of $0.652$, $0.651$ and $0.642$, against $0.481$ for the MLP. The Transformer outperforms the MLP by 0.171, while the difference between the Transformer and GAT+ is not statistically significant. Voids remain the hardest class. Performance is higher in boxes with more selected galaxies and shorter Delaunay edges. Associations with cosmological and feedback parameters weaken after accounting for galaxy richness and void fraction. Our results benchmark the scaling and cross-simulation transfer of galaxy-graph classifiers, demonstrating the value of neighbourhood information and identifying tracer sampling as an important limitation for dark-matter web inference.


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [408] [Decision-Focused Learning for Multistage Stochastic Programs, with Application to Power Network Repair Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34419)
*David Krame Kadurha, Alessandro Zocca, Sandjai Bhulai*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: Multistage stochastic programs (MSPs) with discrete decisions are hard to solve due to the curse of dimensionality, non-convex value functions, and scenario trees that grow exponentially with the horizon. We develop a decision-focused learning (DFL) framework for MSPs in which a neural network predicts scores for the part of the decision that drives the state transition. Under structural conditions that make this sub-decision enter the stage objective linearly, the network is trained on labels from offline single-scenario problems, using the Fenchel-Young loss with perturbed optimizers, which need only an optimization oracle. We apply the framework to repair scheduling in power networks under stochastic, time-varying component failures, with and without a per-stage repair budget. On a synthetic 5-bus network and on the IEEE 14-bus network at two repair-cost scales, the learned and baseline policies are compared on common evaluation paths. Without the budget, DFL is the cheapest policy on the 5-bus network; on the 14-bus network it does not outperform a near-sighted model-predictive control (MPC) baseline, but it beats repairing every failed component at the higher scale. Under a binding budget, DFL is the cheapest policy evaluated on both networks and at both scales, 14% to 23% below the next-best policy. On the 5-bus network, the mean cost of DFL is 1.7% (without the budget) and 1.8% (with it) above the exact optimum computed by dynamic programming. Within days of computation, sample average approximation yields costlier policies than DFL and the repair-everything heuristic, and stochastic dual dynamic integer programming remains far from convergence. DFL computes its decisions about as fast as the trivial heuristics and orders of magnitude faster than MPC. Since these conditions are not specific to power networks, the framework extends to other MSPs that share this structure.


### [409] [Efficient Data Fusion in Distributionally Robust Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34495)
*Buse Şen, Aras Selvi, Jean Pauphilet, Daniel Kuhn*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: Decisions under uncertainty increasingly rest on fragmented evidence, such as samples from related but different populations, privacy-protected summary statistics, or complementary statistical descriptions of the same data. Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) can fuse such evidence by constructing one ambiguity set per source and hedging against all distributions in the intersection of these sets. This fusion approach is less conservative than aggregating the sources into a single ambiguity set, but it is computationally demanding. Exact reformulations grow exponentially with the number of sources, and even deciding whether the intersection is nonempty is NP-hard. We show that fusion DRO admits an exact reformulation in which the ambiguity sets are coupled only through functional Lagrange multipliers. Restricting these multipliers to affine decision rules yields upper bounds that decompose across the ambiguity sets, are exact for quadratic losses over Wasserstein balls around Gaussian references, and provably dominate aggregation-based relaxations such as Minkowski sums and balls around mixtures. The approximations admit finite conic reformulations in important special cases and subgradient methods for weakly convex losses, which makes fusion DRO applicable to neural network training. In portfolio, newsvendor, and binary classification experiments, the affine approximation stays close to the exact value wherever the latter is computable, scales far better than exact methods, and outperforms aggregation-based benchmarks out-of-sample.


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [410] [CypherMAS: Benchmarking Routing Reliability in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33242)
*Danil Kostromin, Andrey Sakhovskiy, Semen Budennyy*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) distribute reasoning across specialized agents that must exchange messages and invoke tools reliably. Existing evaluations typically couple communication with open domain tasks, making it difficult to determine whether a failure arises from reasoning, malformed tool use, lost messages, or forgotten requests. Prior benchmarks do not isolate communication execution or measure routing redundancy against a known optimal path. We introduce CypherMAS, a synthetic benchmark that isolates communication and routing in fixed multi-agent graphs. Agents in chain, star, bipartite, or ring graphs route cyphered messages to remote peers holding the matching decoders; since the optimal route is known, redundant LLM calls can be measured and routing failures may be easily tracked. Across eleven open-weight models from 4B to 753B, small models fail at routing and tool use, dense models above 27B degrade with diameter, and successful runs exceed minimum LLM calls. These findings identify communication reliability as a distinct scaling bottleneck for multi-agent systems and provide a controlled diagnostic for complex multi-agent systems. The source code is freely available: this https URL .


### [411] [On the Tragedy of the Commons in Heterogeneous Distributed Resource Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33172)
*Avadh Kishor, Rajdeep Niyogi*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: We consider the Tragedy of the Commons (ToC) problem, which commonly arises in heterogeneous distributed resource allocation, in which selfish users are independently interested in optimizing their own utility. Such self-interested decision-making in decentralized settings often results in inefficient resource utilization because users prefer high-capacity resources, leading to their overutilization. We cast the problem into a non-cooperative game and derive closed-form characterizations of both the distributed Nash equilibrium (NE) and the socially optimal allocation. In view of these closed-form expressions, we establish the structural conditions under which the ToC arises, and derive an exact expression for the Price of Anarchy (PoA). We further prove that the PoA can become arbitrarily large (unbounded) under suitable traffic conditions. Motivated by these observations, we propose a penalty-based incentive mechanism that discourages the use of overloaded servers while preserving decentralized decision-making. We analyze the resulting equilibrium load redistribution and establish conditions under which the proposed mechanism reduces the overall system cost and improves equilibrium efficiency.


### [412] [When Are Parallel Subagents Effective for Coding? An Empirical Study on Repository Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33893)
*Zhiheng Lyu, Chengyu Yin, Lingming Zhang*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: LLM-based coding agents are increasingly used for long-horizon tasks such as building an entire program from a specification. Products like Codex and Claude Code let an agent delegate work to parallel subagents, multiplying run cost, yet it remains unclear whether and when delegation produces a better program. We compare a single agent with a main agent delegating to subagents on repository-construction tasks from NL2Repo and ProgramBench (which also provides an executable reference program). We compare scores at equal time and equal cost, run intervention experiments, and analyze fine-grained behavior. On randomly drawn tasks, parallel subagents raise hidden-test pass rates by only about 1 to 5 percentage points at up to 3.8x the cost of a single agent. Their gain is larger on hard tasks, where we find evidence of three mechanisms. Self-checking: with GPT-5.6 Sol, one sentence telling a single agent to probe the reference program first recovers most of this gain at a fraction of the cost; from GPT-5.6 Sol to GPT-6.1 Sol, the gain shrinks as single agents check their own work more. Fresh context: in extended runs, a single agent that periodically starts a new session with an empty conversation makes faster early progress in every comparison than one that resumes its conversation. Modularity: multi-agent runs produce more modular code (more files and shorter functions), closer to the upstream source. We observe similar patterns in a smaller replication with Opus 5.5 on Claude Code. Overall, subagents help when (i) a single agent stops checking too early, (ii) a long session keeps editing with little improvement, or (iii) modular code helps on the task; otherwise, they mainly add cost.


### [413] [Post-Training Policy Replication for Strategy-Following Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning across Team Sizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34035)
*Yamato Takahagi, Yoshinari Motokawa, Toshiharu Sugawara*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: Although multi-agent deep reinforcement learning enables coordinated behavior, aligning it with managerial intent remains difficult due to complex agent interactions. Although the strategy-following distributed attentional actor architecture (sfDA7-X) improves controllability via regional instructions, instruction combinations scale exponentially with team and instruction sizes, leading to prohibitive training costs and poor transferability. To reduce this computational cost, we introduce a team-size-independent observation architecture and reconstruct collective behavior by replicating a single trained policy across agents in teams of different sizes, thereby eliminating the need to train from scratch. Additionally, we evaluate warm-start retraining by initializing policy networks with a pre-trained policy to significantly lower computational overhead. In an object collection game, a policy trained in a four-agent team is deployed to teams of three to eight agents. Experiments demonstrate that training with the proposed aggregated observation matrix achieves performance comparable to that of the baseline method, sfDA7-X. Furthermore, policy replication preserves performance without substantial degradation in three- and five-agent teams, and warm-start retraining outperforms training from scratch while reducing required training episodes by up to 90%. Deployments to six- and eight-agent teams further confirm effective spatial region allocation following given instructions. These results demonstrate that the proposed method enables zero-shot collective behavior reconstruction via policy replication.


### [414] [Strategic and Robust Task Allocation for Combat Objectives in Adversarial Multi-Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34158)
*Jonas Peché, Dmitry Matveev, Vladimir Ciric, Lizaveta Sakalova, Günter Wallner*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: Task allocation in contested-objective team games requires coordinated decisions under uncertainty, spatial constraints, and changing tactical conditions. In such settings, multiple agents must anticipate both teammate behavior and opponent actions. We study a hybrid predict-then-optimize approach for assigning controllable agents to objectives in real time. A masked multi-head model predicts tactical signals for each agent, including survival chance, local superiority, combat interaction, travel time, and objective contribution. These signals are combined with rule-based terms in a score matrix, and a minimum-cost flow solver computes coordinated global assignments constrained by capacity, occupancy, stability, and defensive strategies. Learning is limited to tactical scoring, while explicit rules and solver structure preserve controllability and interpretability. We evaluate the system using predictive metrics, offline assignment diagnostics, online A/B evaluation, and latency measurements. The system is an R\&D research prototype built on a commercial game and is not deployed in the shipped game. The results indicate that learned tactical estimates can support stable, feasible, and inspectable objective allocation under strict real-time constraints.


### [415] [Soft Decentralized Expected SARSA: Equilibrium Selection via Gradual Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33228)
*Giuseppe Martucci, Janusz M. Meylahn, Gurdal Arslan, Serdar Yuksel*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: We introduce Soft Decentralized Expected SARSA (SDES), a modification of Decentralized Q-learning in which agents maintain persistent exploration and update their acting Q-values gradually rather than switching directly to newly learned best responses when updating. We prove convergence to $\epsilon$-greedy Nash equilibria in weakly acyclic stochastic games for sufficiently large batch sizes. We then show, in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with memory, that soft updating can reshape basins of attraction without changing the equilibrium set. In the deterministic limit, we derive analytical basin bounds governed by the Q-value differences between actions in the target policies and show that reducing the policy learning rate can favor either cooperative or competitive equilibria. These results suggest that soft updating is not merely a stabilization device but also a mechanism of equilibrium selection in decentralized learning.


### [416] [What Must LLM Agents Communicate? From Information Loss to Communication Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33318)
*Yi-Yun Chu, Xiaobin Shen*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: When evidence is distributed across LLM agents, a team may collectively possess all the information needed for a decision yet fail to use it effectively. What must agents communicate for accurate joint predictions, and when does interaction help? We study three requirements for effective communication: sufficiency of the information conveyed, selection of relevant evidence, and utilization of received information. Drawing on information theory and communication complexity, we develop a framework that combines theoretical benchmarks with controlled experiments and message-level analyses. We find that local probability reports can discard essential information, with theoretical information loss closely matching observed performance penalties. Query-first interaction improves predictions when relevant evidence depends on a private query, but compact one-shot representations can eliminate this advantage. Even when sufficient information is communicated, agents may fail to select or use it correctly. Together, these findings distinguish fundamental communication limits from failures in how LLM agents communicate and use information.


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [417] [Hierarchical Semantic Learning for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34184)
*Chunlei Meng, Rong Fu, Zhan Chen, Yi Liu, Zhuo Zou, LiDa Xu, Chun Ouyang, Zhongxue Gan*

Main category: cs.MM

Abstract: Multimodal sentiment analysis (MSA) in real-world systems must handle unpredictable modality missingness caused by sensor failures, privacy masking, and unstable transmission. Existing methods either learn reliability-aware representations or reconstruct missing inputs, often using a single flattened encoder output. This overlooks deep encoders' semantic hierarchy and intra- and inter-modal interactions, causing semantic averaging and ill-posed mappings. We propose Hierarchical Semantic Learning (HSL), a framework that exploits semantic hierarchy for robust MSA with missing modalities. Hierarchical Semantic Decoupling exposes low-, mid-, and high-level feature streams from each modality encoder. Cross-Modal Cross-Level Semantic Reconstruction performs ladder-style recovery, using high-level semantics from observed modalities as global anchors to progressively reconstruct mid-level structures and low-level details of missing modalities. To stabilize cross-level reconstruction, Hierarchical Cascade Error Supervisor applies prototypical contrastive regularization to observed and reconstructed features, enforcing level-specific semantic consistency and limiting error propagation. Finally, Hierarchical Interactive Fusion captures dense interactions across modalities and semantic levels to produce robust predictions under diverse missingness patterns. Experiments on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, IEMOCAP, and CH-SIMS show that HSL outperforms baselines in most missing-modality settings.


### [418] [Can a Short Video Clip Verify a Model Fix?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33393)
*Raj Shan*

Main category: cs.MM

Abstract: A developer who reproduces a video model's error on a short clip can replay that clip after an update. How often does a correct clip answer mean the original test now passes? On 960 prespecified CG-Bench questions, we updated from Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct to Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct. Accuracy on a 64-frame sample spanning the full video rose from 27.9% to 33.3%. Before scoring the newer model, we froze 388 clips on which the older model reproduced its exact reference error. The updated model passed the reference on 51 of 116 clip passes (44.0%, 95% Wilson interval 35.3-53.0%) and on 16 of 272 clip failures (5.9%). Closing cases on a clip pass would have declared 65 unresolved reference failures fixed. Requiring a clip pass would have missed 16 of the 67 reference fixes (23.9%). In a separate 1,028-video study with different checkpoints and direct option scoring, only 15 clips passed, and the 16-frame reference confirmed one. For the generated-answer update, the short replay identified cases worth checking, while the original input remained necessary to verify individual fixes.


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [419] [Data-free continuous-time physics-informed neural networks for vortex shedding: a consistent pressure across time windows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33489)
*Mickaël Delcey, Cyril Regan, Masaki Fuchiwaki, Yoann Cheny*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

Abstract: Without data, physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) in continuous time have so far failed to develop vortex shedding past a circular cylinder in a domain symmetric about the wake centerline: the training converges to the steady symmetric solution. We obtain shedding at Re=100 with a continuous-time PINN trained over successive time windows, without any reference data and without temporal discretization, the symmetry being broken by an analytical, divergence-free initial condition. The Strouhal number and the mean drag coefficient agree with an OpenFOAM solution computed on the same domain to within 0.5%. The pressure, however, is discontinuous at the junctions between windows, and the lift coefficient is a staircase. We relate this discontinuity to the differential-algebraic structure of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, of index 2 after spatial discretization: a consistent initial condition of each window must satisfy the constraint $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{v}=0$ for the velocity, and the hidden constraint $\nabla\cdot\partial_t\mathbf{v}=0$, with $\partial_t\mathbf{v}$ given by the momentum equation, then determines the pressure. Imposing the pressure alone makes the solution continuous but not consistent; our remedy imposes the pressure at the junctions and adds this hidden constraint to the loss. Over five shedding periods, the jumps of the lift coefficient become 20 times smaller.


<div id='cs.CY'></div>

# cs.CY [[Back]](#toc)

### [420] [The AI Disclosure Paradox: Operational Disclosure and the Rise of Corporate AI Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33201)
*Abdullah Karasan*

Main category: cs.CY

Abstract: AI-related corporate disclosure can expand while becoming less concentrated on current use. Across U.S. SEC filings from 2017 to 2025, AI-related passage intensity increased 6.3-fold between 2017-2022 and 2023-2025, whereas corrected operational-disclosure intensity increased 3.3-fold. The operational share fell from 25.76% to 13.45%; approximately 89% of the net increase in passage intensity fell outside the operational category. The decline persists within sectors and filing types and in 10-K-only analysis. These estimates combine frozen language-model predictions for 26,279 AI-related passages with an audit of 1,000 annotated passages. The findings distinguish growth in organizational attention from growth in implementation-specific claims: more operational disclosure can coexist with a substantially lower operational share. Operational disclosure was thus diluted, not displaced, by nonoperational disclosure. Reading corporate technology narratives therefore requires attention to both disclosure intensity and the organizational state described.


### [421] [The Socratic PAUSE: safeguarding scientific judgement at AI speed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34222)
*Abdulkadir Celik, Sir Bashir M. Al-Hashimi*

Main category: cs.CY

Abstract: Generative AI can turn a tentative scientific question into hypotheses, methods, code and interpretations within minutes. Yet generating a contribution does not establish that it deserves scientific reliance: researchers must still examine its evidence, assumptions and implications, and remain accountable for the decision to rely on it. Using Einstein's sketch of scientific discovery as a lens, we argue that AI has accelerated discovery far more than justification. In human-AI research partnerships, confidence can therefore outgrow evidence through three mechanisms: recursive reinforcement, loss of provenance and miscalibrated reliance on AI-generated contributions. Verification debt accumulates when such contributions are carried into later claims or decisions without adequate scrutiny. Accordingly, we propose the Socratic Pause as a deliberate checkpoint at consequential decision gates, where an AI contribution remains provisional while a researcher examines its basis. The PAUSE protocol structures this examination through five steps: Pin down the claim, Ask and challenge, Uncover assumptions and alternatives, Scrutinise the evidence and Exercise judgement. It concludes with a recorded decision to accept, revise, reject or defer the contribution. We show how AI research tools can support this practice without displacing human judgement, and outline a framework for evaluating whether it improves verification, decision-making and independent understanding. The aim is to preserve the freedom to explore with AI while ensuring that those responsible for the work can say what was examined, what remains uncertain and why the next step is justified.


### [422] [The cost of being seen as AI in group bargaining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34322)
*Tahmineh Godazgar, Nico Mutzner, Taha Yasseri*

Main category: cs.CY

Abstract: As artificial intelligence (AI) becomes embedded in collective decision-making, people's perceptions of AI may shape who reaches agreement and what they receive. We studied this in a repeated three-player majoritarian bargaining game with 2,234 participants. Players saw each opponent identified as Human or AI. The instructions described AI as acting either independently or on behalf of a human. Human participants made every decision. In mixed groups, players presented as AI received fewer points in opening offers, were included in fewer final agreements and earned about 18% less per round than those presented as Human. Smaller payoffs in agreements that included them accounted for most of the observed gap. Further analysis found a payoff gap even when all three players agreed. Social perceptions of AI may therefore shape who benefits from collective decisions, with potential costs for people represented by AI.


<div id='eess.SY'></div>

# eess.SY [[Back]](#toc)

### [423] [One Neural Operator for Re-planning Low-Thrust Cislunar Transfers after Thrust Outages, at the Original or a Later Arrival](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33188)
*Selim Olgu Pilav*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: Safe-mode events causing thrust outages are common in missions with electric propulsion. To demonstrate mission feasibility in such an event, missed-thrust margin studies are performed where thousands of outage scenarios need an exact re-plan solved. We train a neural operator that maps a nominal trajectory with an outage profile to a re-planned trajectory and to the probability that the recovery exists, for 270 low-thrust near-rectilinear halo orbit (NRHO) to distant retrograde orbit (DRO) transfers, at an allowed slip of up to 10 days. On unseen transfers and zero slip, its median control error on the severe half of outages is 1.20% compared to 14.0% for the best cheap re-planner benchmark. With the arrival slip as an input, the error is 1.67% against 23.2%. It also predicts the recoverability of an outage for a given arrival slip with an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.997, while the minimum recoverable slip prediction is optimistic by more than half a day on only 6 of the 84 checked outages needing slip.
