At the original arrival, the ensemble spread of an operator variant with solver inputs has a rank correlation of 0.91 with the actual error, and performing exact solves on the 31% most uncertain severe outages reduces the 90th-percentile error from 8.5% to 2.3%. A query takes milliseconds without a solver, making this a useful tool for large outage studies where the cost of thousands of exact solves outweighs the time to train the operator.


### [424] [Online Identification Can Replace a Floor-Plan Prior on Unseen Buildings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33656)
*Elias T.-H. Shieh, Chun Fu*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: The Smart Buildings Challenge asks for one HVAC control policy that runs office buildings and weather it has never seen, from the building's metadata and sensor streams alone. Our controller sees no data from the target building before it runs and is not tuned per building; its few global constants were set on development scenarios. Each zone has a first-order grey-box model that starts from coefficients computed from the floor plan and is updated online by recursive least squares, and a planner and a closed-form mapping turn the models into plant commands. On two sets of buildings and weather that we generated, a validation set and a stress set with real-year weather, it scores 0.145 and 0.159 times the starter kit's PID baseline, and 0.095 times on a holdout built after all versions were fixed and scored once. It improves on every building, also against our two implementations of ASHRAE Guideline 36: per set it scores 0.60 to 0.71 times a basic version and 0.83 to 0.96 times one partly tuned on the public leaderboard, which wins one of 54 scenarios; with a 5 °C frost target for empty zones on both, the margin over the tuned version shrinks to 1.4% on the stress set, where one of six buildings is 0.2% worse. In an ablation on a reduced-order model, removing both the floor-plan prior and online identification costs 28%, 20% and 42% on the validation, stress and holdout sets, far more than the two effects combined: either source of building-specific information recovers most of what is lost without both. On one office floor in a second simulator, BOPTEST, this does not carry over: with identification on, the two priors give similar results, but the floor-plan prior alone, with constants fitted on the competition's simulator, is the least stable arm. These simulation results motivate testing floor-plan-initialised adaptive control in buildings that have drawings but no operating data.


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [425] [Privacy-Aware Adaptive Client Selection for Federated Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33890)
*Ali Shams Saryazd, Abolfazl Gandomi*

Main category: cs.IR

Abstract: Federated retrieval-augmented generation retrieves evidence from distributed corpora. However, broadcasting queries to all clients increases the retrieval burden and query exposure. We present Privacy-Aware Adaptive Client Selection for Federated Retrieval-Augmented Generation (PACS-FedRAG), an online selector that takes into account perturbed semantic profiles along with evidence feedback, latency, payload cost, and exploration. In a ten-client setting where three clients are chosen, the archived end-to-end benchmark reports an evidence overlap of 0.372 and an answer accuracy of 0.287. The retrieval-only evaluation, on the other hand, reuses a full-broadcast baseline with a different fusion tie-breaking policy. Rank fusion across disjoint client corpora imposes an overlap ceiling of 0.375, limiting its interpretation as a relevance measure. Answer accuracy is below the fixed-label diagnostic of 0.645, and raw generator responses are unavailable for parser verification. Corrected baselines and a new answer-level evaluation are needed to assess whether quality is preserved. Selecting three clients reduces the number of query recipients by 70%. Profile perturbation does not provide confidentiality of queries or passages.


### [426] [ConTra: A Training Framework for Effective Contextual Retrieval via Prefix Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34366)
*Jiajun Hou, Jingbang Chen*

Main category: cs.IR

Abstract: Data management systems organize large text collections for tasks such as finding relevant studies and extracting contract clauses. Systems make long documents searchable by splitting them into independently indexed chunks. Chunking can separate text from its identifying details. The indexing challenge is to keep each chunk independently searchable while preserving the context needed to find and rank it. Existing methods either encode that context in chunk vectors or attach it as a text prefix. Context encoded only in vectors is unavailable to text rerankers. Prompted prefixes serve both vector search and reranking, but are costly to generate at indexing time and can underperform contextual encoders. We propose ConTra (Context That Travels), a reinforcement learning framework for training lightweight prefix generators. Its reward combines feedback from frozen embedding and reranking models with factual verification and length control. ConTra generates one prefix per chunk during index construction and stores the augmented text and its embedding for reuse across queries. To evaluate contextual retrieval across workloads and under interference, we introduce RealContextBench, a benchmark with five application collections and three controlled conditions that isolate the effect of distracting text. On ConTEB and RealContextBench, ConTra-2B outperforms the prompted 9B baseline using Anthropic's method and the PPLX-4B contextual encoder, with a margin of up to 3.15 points over their best overall scores, while indexing short documents 7.74 times and 2.15 times as fast as those baselines, respectively. Post-trained only on ConTEB, it leads the standalone methods on RealContextBench after reranking and is less sensitive than the prompted 2B baseline to random and topically similar distractors. Code, datasets, and trained models are available at this https URL .


### [427] [Learning the Platform, Not the Learner: Sequencing Shortcuts in the Offline Evaluation of Educational Recommender Systems and LLM Rankers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33240)
*Youssef Jdidou*

Main category: cs.IR

Abstract: Educational recommenders are usually compared offline, by how well they predict the next activity in logs of tutoring systems, learning applications and MOOCs, where the next activity is largely decided by the platform: teachers assign problem sets, tutors draw problems until mastery, engines select questions. On eight public datasets from five platforms, we compare four structural predictors, which use nothing about the learner beyond the last activity, with ten personalised recommenders and with open-weight LLM rankers from 0.5 to 7 billion parameters, under two protocols. The structural predictors reach between 0.81 and 1.00 of the best accuracy of any method (median 0.95), mostly through population transitions rather than the course order; the best personalised recommender is significantly better on five datasets, by 0.007 to 0.102 NDCG@10, and on Eedi in three of four training runs. Every method is far less accurate on steps along transitions never seen in training and on steps chosen by learners. LLM rankers look competent with random negatives but are never much better than a word-matching baseline, and with negatives that are plausible under the course structure they stay close to random or well behind the transition heuristic, whatever their size. In a simulation with known learner needs, the structural share tracks the share of steps decided by the platform, and offline accuracy ranks an oracle of the learners' needs below imitators of the platform. Weighting test steps by how unlikely the structure makes them restores much of the link with usefulness and, on the real data, reveals a significant personalisation signal on seven of the eight datasets. We propose a syllabus-aware evaluation checklist and release a Python package that reports the lift of any recommender over the structure.


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [428] [Beat This! Live - Online Beat and Downbeat Tracking by Predicting the Future](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34277)
*Bánk Juhász, Gerhard Widmer, Jan Schlüter*

Main category: cs.SD

Abstract: Beat and downbeat tracking have seen multiple improvements through recent deep learning approaches, but most target an offline setting, precluding their use in real-time scenarios. We adapt a recent offline beat tracking model, Beat This, for online, low-latency operation and propose Beat This! Live, a causal variant designed for real-time beat and downbeat tracking. We present the architectural modifications required to obtain a causal system suitable for streaming inference and measure their impact on accuracy. By training our model with temporally shifted labels, it produces predictions slightly ahead of the current frame, which not only reduces latency, but enables much more effective peak-picking. Evaluation on the GTZAN dataset shows the F1-score improves from 80.6 to 86.7 for beats and 56.5 to 74.1 for downbeats compared to BeatNet+, the current state of the art in online beat tracking.


### [429] [Music Similarity Estimation from Jukebox Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34346)
*Johannes Grafinger, Gerhard Widmer, Jan Schlüter*

Main category: cs.SD

Abstract: Content-based music similarity estimation aims to provide a measure of similarity between music audio recordings, disregarding any associated metadata. This has potential applications in e.g. cold-start music recommendation scenarios, where there is no user interaction data. In this work, we propose JukeSim, a new approach to estimating musical similarity based on embeddings created by Jukebox, a generative music model. In evaluations on multiple datasets, we find that JukeSim performs on par with an existing learning-based approach, and both outperform the best hand-designed approaches, which had never been compared to recent work before. Even though the required compute for JukeSim is higher than for other measures, we demonstrate the viability of utilizing generative embeddings for this task, encouraging further research.


### [430] [A Head-Level Investigative Study of Self-Supervised Backbones for Synthetic Speech Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34426)
*Mohamed Mallat, Michele Panariello, Massimiliano Todisco, Anthony Larcher, Nicholas Evans*

Main category: cs.SD

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) speech backbones are the standard frontend for synthetic-speech detection, and layer-wise probing has established which layers carry the most signal. But analysis stops there: which of a layer's twelve attention heads compute the spoof decision is unknown. We present the first head-level study of two frozen backbones, WavLM-base and Wav2Vec2-base, on ASVspoof 5. Per-head ablation shows that only a few heads carry the decision while others actively oppose it; removing those heads lowers equal error rate (EER) with no retraining, by 13% relative in WavLM and 22% in Wav2Vec2. We also find a probe-free signature: spoofed speech draws more focused attention than genuine speech, and this gap alone separates the two almost as well as the full representation, peaking at each backbone's most discriminative layer. Finally, attack identity is decodable from every head, yet heads differ in what they do, not in what they know.


<div id='stat.ML'></div>

# stat.ML [[Back]](#toc)

### [431] [The Symmetry Budget: How Much Group Structure Should a Network Architecture Assume?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33169)
*Noor Islam S. Mohammad, Mahmudul Hasan, Md. Basim Al Zabir Shammo, Hasan Siddiki, Jakaria Habib*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: A network architecture is, in large part, a symmetry assumption: convolution ties weights across translations, attention ties them across permutations, and a fully connected layer ties nothing. When the data are only approximately symmetric, tying trades approximation error against estimation error, and the right amount of tying is a property of the dataset, not of the architecture alone. We study this trade-off in an exactly solvable model: linear maps on signals over the cyclic group $\mathbb{Z}_d$, with architectures indexed by the subgroup lattice of $\mathbb{Z}_d$, so that a single integer $k \mid d$ interpolates between an unconstrained layer ($k=1$, $d^2$ parameters) and a fully equivariant one ($k=d$, $d$ parameters). We derive the finite-sample excess risk in closed form, show that for a generic symmetry defect the leading-order risk is affine in $1/k$, and prove that intermediate architectures are then never strictly optimal: the lattice collapses to an all-or-nothing choice with phase boundary $n^\star = \sigma^2 d(d-1)/\varepsilon^2$. Structured defects break this collapse and restore interior optima, and we give a closed-form defect profile for a graded defect. Measurements agree with the theory on $60/60$ cells of a $(\varepsilon,n)$ phase diagram and $48/48$ cells of a second phase diagram at $d=16$, recover the predicted exponent as $-1.92$ against $-2$, and exhibit the predicted $k^\star: 12 \to 4 \to 1$ ladder under graded defects; a two-layer nonlinear control reproduces the crossover. We also characterize when the affine collapse survives for non-abelian groups.


### [432] [A Causal Energy Prior for Learning Directed Acyclic Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33225)
*Marco Scutari, Noriaki Sato, Shuichi Kawano, Rui Yamaguchi, Seiya Imoto*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Bayesian learning of directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) has long relied on non- or weakly informative priors, on the assumption that the information needed for an informative one is rarely available. In genetics and systems biology, annotation databases now make it possible to assemble a prior DAG before learning from data. Existing informative priors are local: they score a DAG arc by arc and cannot encode how interventions propagate along its paths. Thus, we propose the local-global energy prior (LGE), a Gibbs distribution over DAGs whose energy combines a local divergence from the prior DAG, the Structural Hamming Distance, and a causal one, the Structural Intervention Distance. We derive rules to calibrate its decay and the weight of the two divergences from the prior DAG alone. We quantify how much prior mass stays on the true DAG, and how much data overturn the prior, when the prior DAG is partially incorrect. In simulations, LGE improves both the structural accuracy of maximum a posteriori DAGs and the fit of the corresponding Bayesian networks to the true distribution over non- and weakly informative priors, even when half of the arcs of the prior DAG are wrong. We also showcase LGE on a gene regulatory network learned from single-cell kidney data.


### [433] [Evaluating Ensemble Methods for Algorithmic Stability Under Data Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33375)
*Benjamin Edelhertz, Simon D. Nguyen, Tyler H. McCormick*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Standard classification algorithms often exhibit sensitivity near decision boundaries, which can compound instability that already exists from overfitting to a subset of high-influence samples. Ensembling is commonly used to stabilize classifiers, but we show that it can destabilize those that are already stable. The well-established practice of subbagging can yield substantial improvements in stability, as we validate in the cause-of-death classification setting. However, its effect depends on the perturbation level, and for stable classifiers the finite number of bags introduces destabilizing Monte Carlo noise that shrinks only slowly as the number of bags grows. This instability can be mitigated, though, by the recently proposed inflated argmax, which allows for multi-class predictions in ambiguous cases. We further examine the extent to which the Rashomon set, the collection of distinct models that achieve near-optimal predictive performance, can reduce instability by leveraging predictive multiplicity rather than resampling. We find that the Rashomon ensemble can stabilize unstable classifiers, but requires sufficient predictive multiplicity, which is not guaranteed. It is a promising direction, but not currently a general substitute for subbagging. Our results are consistent with an influence equalization interpretation of why these ensemble approaches stabilize unstable classifiers, although their failures on stable classifiers appear to have distinct sources.


### [434] [Gaussian priors from nonlinear PDEs with the semiclassical approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33400)
*Alex Alberts*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Probabilistic numerical methods for partial differential equations (PDE) combines the governing physics with observations to treat solving the PDE as a Bayesian inverse problem. This allows techniques to quantify the uncertainty in the field reconstruction under model-form error. In the majority of cases, a Gaussian prior is assigned to the field of interest, and Bayes's theorem in the infinite-dimensional setting is used to derive the posterior. While Gaussians have been shown to be well-suited for linear PDEs, a similar treatment for nonlinear PDEs remains open-ended. In this work, study the use of the semiclassical approximation to construct Gaussian priors from variational energies or integrated square residuals for probabilistic numerics. The prior is derived by expanding the PDE energy about a stable solution and uncovering a Gaussian form. We then analyze the different characteristics of the resulting priors as they pertain to the inverse problem. In particular, we find that the covariance operator is given by the inverse of the Hessian of the energy whose associated Cameron-Martin space is the solution space of the PDE. We also find that the prior responds to model-form error by sharpening or flattening itself depending on the distance between the ground truth and the solution of the assumed model.


### [435] [Centered Kernel Distance: Dimension-Free Error Bounds for Comparing Neural Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33447)
*Shujun Xiong, Sarah E. Harvey, Jacob Zavatone-Veth, Alexander H. Williams*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: The asymptotic error of representational similarity metrics such as centered kernel alignment (CKA) and representational similarity analysis (RSA) has been well-characterized, but there are no realistic bounds on how estimates of representational similarity $\textit{simultaneously}$ depend on the number of sampled neurons ($N$) and experimental conditions ($M$). We study the natural metric counterpart of CKA and RSA, which we call the Centered Kernel Distance (CKD). We show that CKD admits an unbiased estimator, whereas several popular similarity measures including CKA have no unbiased estimator. Under mild assumptions, we show that estimation error of CKD decays in proportion to $1/M + 1/N$. Strikingly, the rate in this bound does not depend on the dimensionality of the space of experimental conditions. Thus, similarity estimation does not suffer from the curse of dimensionality, which plagues similar problems in high-dimensional statistics. We verify our results empirically in random shallow neural networks, trained neural networks, and macaque visual cortex data. Ultimately, this paper advances a framework for predicting the error of similarity estimators in experiments by relaxing existing, biologically-unrealistic assumptions. Our theoretical and empirical predictions produce specific prescriptions on how to most efficiently estimate the similarity between multiple neural systems.


### [436] [PAC Learning to Sample: On Computational Aspects of Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33464)
*Alkis Kalavasis, Shuchen Li, Manolis Zampetakis*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: The success of language models has motivated theoretical work on learning to sample from distributions on discrete domains. A prominent abstraction is the model of language generation in the limit, introduced by Kleinberg and Mullainathan (NeurIPS 2024), in which the algorithm must generate from the target language after some finite but unspecified number of positive examples. While its positive results are surprisingly general, most guarantees are qualitative. In this work, we seek quantitative guarantees on sample and time complexity, inspired by statistical and computational learning theory. To this end, we study a PAC model of language generation, proposed (in a different context) by De, Diakonikolas, and Servedio (SODA 2015), in which a Boolean concept class $C$ over $\{-1,1\}^n$ defines the target languages: given uniformly random positive examples of an unknown concept $f \in C$, the learner must output a sampler whose output distribution (conditioned on the training data) is close in total variation to the uniform distribution over $f^{-1}(1)$. Their work implicitly gives an efficient algorithm for PAC learning to sample $C$, given three primitives for $C$: an online learner, approximate sampling and counting oracles, and an SQ learner. We ask whether these ingredients, in particular online learnability, are necessary. First, we construct a Boolean concept class $C$ for which online learning is cryptographically hard, yet PAC learning to sample is computationally efficient. Then, we develop new algorithms that use offline ERM oracles instead of online learners, together with approximate sampling/counting oracles for intersections of concepts in $C$ and an SQ learner for $C$. We view our results, together with the work of De, Diakonikolas, and Servedio, as a first step towards understanding the computational complexity of language generation.


### [437] [On the Impact of Extra Information in Bandit Learning: More is Not Always Merrier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33479)
*Ramakrishnan Krishnamurthy*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: More data is commonly assumed to improve learning performance, however, we show that this intuition can surprisngly fail in sequential decision-making. Considering a stochastic bandits setup with exogenous extra information, we ask if the free and unbiased extra reward samples are guaranteed to reduce the regret of the learner. We introduce a formal notion of `non-degradation under extra information' and demonstrate that the widely used Upper Confidence Bound algorithm (UCB) violates this property, i.e., there exist benign extra-information under which UCB incurs strictly higher regret than when run without extra information. We contrast this behaviour with Thompson Sampling and Explore-Then-Commit, which appear to benefit from the same extra information. Constructively, we propose a meta-algorithm that provably enforces non-degradation for a broad class of bandit algorithms. We also run empirical simulations to study our meta-algorithm.


### [438] [Adversarial Bandit Convex Optimization with $d^{3/2}\sqrt{n}$ Regret](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33531)
*Alireza Bakhtiari, Kevin Jamieson, Tor Lattimore, Lillian Ratliff*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study adversarial bandit convex optimization, where in each of $n$ rounds a learner chooses a point $A_t$ in a convex body $\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^d$ and observes only a noisy value of a convex loss $f_t:\mathcal{K}\to[0,1]$ at that point. The losses are chosen by an adversary, who may change them from round to round and adapt them to the learner's past actions. We improve the best known regret bound for this problem from $\widetilde O(d^{5/2}\sqrt n)$ (Lattimore, 2020, 2026) to $\widetilde O(d^{3/2}\sqrt n)$, which matches the rate conjectured by Bubeck et al. (2017). Together with the recent lower bound of $\Omega(d^{4/3}\sqrt n)$ due to Rajaraman and Han (2026), it determines the minimax regret up to a factor of $d^{1/6}$ and logarithmic factors. The algorithm is an online Newton step with Gaussian exploration. Unlike earlier methods of this kind, it uses unbiased estimates of the gradient and Hessian of the Gaussian smoothing of the loss, rather than of an optimistic surrogate.


### [439] [Improved Sample Complexity of Robust Reinforcement Learning with KL Uncertainty Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33592)
*Yudan Wang, Zilong Deng, Nathaniel D. Bastian, Shaofeng Zou*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: In this paper, we study distributionally robust reinforcement learning with Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence-defined uncertainty sets. The goal is to find a policy that maximizes the robust value function, defined as the worst-case value over all transition kernels in the uncertainty set. Assuming access to a generative model, we aim to understand the sample complexity of finding an $\epsilon$-optimal robust policy. The best-known sample complexity results in the literature show a non-trivial gap of $\mathcal{O}\{\max\{p_\wedge^{-1}(1-\gamma)^{-1},(1-\gamma)^{-2}\}\}$ between the upper and lower bounds, where $p_\wedge$ denotes the minimum positive transition probability of the nominal transition kernel, and $\gamma$ is the discount factor. More importantly, existing results on the lower bound only cover a limited range of the uncertainty level. In this paper, we develop tighter and complete upper and lower bounds for robust RL with KL-defined uncertainty sets. Our upper bound is the tightest among all existing studies, and it improves upon the best-known bound by at least the order of $\mathcal{O}(\min\{p_\wedge^{-1},(1-\gamma)^{-1}\})$ for uncertainty level $\delta\in(0,1)$. Furthermore, our lower bound holds for any uncertainty level. Our upper and lower bounds (nearly) match in various regimes, providing improved results for this problem.


### [440] [Understanding Double Descent through Universal Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33759)
*Jiaxuan Cheng, Addison Spiegel*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Many deep learning models exhibit a double-descent phenomenon, where test loss exhibits a sharp peak near the interpolation threshold and then descends a second time as model size grows further, contrary to the U-shape predicted by the classical bias--variance tradeoff. The double-descent phenomenon has been found and explained in toy classical models, but is not well-understood in modern deep learning despite extensive empirical study. We interpret double descent through the novel lens of universal compression. Specifically, we upper bound the maximum-likelihood excess test loss as a sum of four terms: an approximation-and-shift term, a minimax batch regret, a prior cost, and an MLE-vs-Bayes gap. Under exact posterior sampling, we further show that the expected MLE-vs-ensemble gap lower-bounds the MLE-vs-Bayes gap in the well-specified setting. In our experiments, a Gaussian-prior SGLD approximation to the Bayes predictor, unlike SGD, shows no pronounced interpolation peak. Experiments with uniform random labels support this gap as a major contributor to the double-descent peak; here, the SGD cross-entropy is roughly four times the SGLD ensemble cross-entropy at the peak, while the ensemble cross-entropy remains near $\log 10$.


### [441] [RHOPE: LLM-Augmented Adaptive Experimental Design with Complex Contextual Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33767)
*Bowen Shi, Bo Li*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Experimental design allocates limited measurement effort across candidate treatments to learn their expected outcomes. Adaptive designs refine allocations as evidence accumulates, improving estimation and treatment selection. Contextual information can inform these decisions, but exploiting unstructured or high-dimensional context remains challenging. We use large language models (LLMs) to transform complex context into proxy outcomes and propose Residual Horizon Optimization for Prediction-Powered Experimentation (RHOPE), a principled Bayesian adaptive experimentation framework that combines true outcomes with LLM-generated proxy outcomes and formulates finite-batch design as a posterior Markov decision process (MDP). Our methodology uses proxy outcomes in two ways: sample augmentation improves estimation precision, while observing proxies before each allocation decision provides advance information. Synthetic experiments and semi-synthetic experiments using Genomics of Drug Sensitivity in Cancer (GDSC) data suggest that estimation precision drives most gains, with smaller advance-information gains, and highlight the importance of proxy informativeness and moment-estimation accuracy.


### [442] [A Graphical Criterion for Linear Causal Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33950)
*Alexis Bellot*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: The values of causal effects and counterfactuals are typically only partially identified from observational data in the presence of unobserved confounding. With discretely-valued variables, under certain conditions, sharp bounds can be derived by solving one linear program. This is then computationally tractable for many causal questions. In this paper, for every diagram in which each confounded component has at most two hidden causes, we give necessary and sufficient conditions for the sharp bounds to be equal to the optimum of one linear program for every (positive) input probability table. This algorithm is implemented in a new Python package, called "partialid", that allows users to compose assumptions, queries, and data to obtain sharp bounds in a computationally tractable manner.


### [443] [Accelerated Proximal Gradient Methods for Multiobjective Optimization over Probability Measures via Hilbert-Space Lifting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34073)
*Dai Hai Nguyen*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Many sampling and inference tasks require a single distribution that balances several targets. We formulate this task as multiobjective optimization of Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergences over probability measures, in the Pareto sense. The particle method MWGraD (Nguyen et al., 2025) uses explicit Wasserstein gradient steps, and its accelerated version A-MWGraD (Nguyen et al., 2026) lacks discrete-time guarantees. We propose the multiobjective Wasserstein proximal gradient (MWPG), which uses implicit Wasserstein gradient steps, and its accelerated version, multiobjective Wasserstein accelerated proximal gradient (MWAPG), with rigorous discrete-time guarantees. Our key idea is a Hilbert-space lifting: each measure is represented as the pushforward of a fixed base measure by a map in a Hilbert space. The problem then becomes a constrained multiobjective composite problem over transport maps, to which accelerated proximal gradient methods apply. For convex and smooth potentials, we show that MWAPG decreases a Pareto merit function at the rate $\mathcal{O}(1/k^2)$; for $m$-strongly convex and $L$-smooth potentials, the rate is $\mathcal{O}\big((1-\sqrt{m/L})^k\big)$. We also give closed-form Gaussian updates and particle implementations based on input-convex neural networks, which require no score estimation. Experiments on synthetic and real-world dataset show that MWAPG needs far fewer iterations than MWPG and matches or outperforms A-MWGraD in Bayesian multi-task learning.


### [444] [Conditional Independence Graph Learning from Dependent Data Via Time-Delay Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34616)
*Jitendra K. Tugnait*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We consider the problem of inferring the conditional independence graph (CIG) of a high-dimensional stationary multivariate Gaussian time series which could be short-range dependent (SRD) or long-range dependent (LRD). In a time series graph, each component of the vector series is represented by a distinct node, and associations between components are represented by edges between the corresponding nodes. We consider a time-domain approach based on time-delay embedding, casting the problem as one of multi-attribute graph estimation for random vectors where a vector is associated with each node of the graph. For an embedding dimension $d$, at each node the associated $(d+1)$-dimensional random vector consists of a time series component and its $d$ delayed copies, with integer delays up to $d \ge 1$. Graph estimation from dependent data has been investigated in the past using frequency-domain methods whose analysis is restricted to SRD data. We consider a penalized negative pseudo log-likelihood function with convex lasso or non-convex regularizations to estimate the CIG. Non-convex regularization functions are log-sum, smoothly clipped absolute deviation (SCAD) and minimax concave penalty (MCP). We exploit an existing alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) approach to optimize the objective function. We establish sufficient conditions in a high-dimensional setting for consistency, local convexity when using non-convex penalties, and graph recovery, for both SRD and LRD series. We illustrate our approach with both synthetic and real-data examples. For a $p$-dimensional time series, the computational complexity of the ADMM algorithm is ${\cal O}((d+1)^3 p^3)$. Based on the numerical examples, a heuristic is suggested and illustrated for selection of $d$ which, in theory, should tend to infinity to infer the time series graph.

### [445] [Probabilistic Machine Learning for Total Kinetic Energy Distributions in Neutron-Induced Fission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33379)
*Vaia Prassa, Martha Papadopoulou, Christos Bompotas*

Main category: nucl-th

Abstract: The incident-neutron-energy dependence of post-neutron total-kinetic-energy (TKE) distributions, particularly their width in the multichance-fission regime, provides a sensitive test of fission calculations. We investigate how the General Description of Fission Observables (GEF) and probabilistic machine-learning models trained on GEF reproduce measured TKE distributions, emphasizing their width at high incident-neutron energies and extrapolation beyond the training range. Gaussian process regression (GPR), GPR with a white-noise kernel, a mixture density network (MDN), and a Bayesian mixture density network (BMDN) are evaluated on a common calculated dataset. An extended MDN/BMDN analysis uses 1,025 GEF spectra from 20 target nuclei and 60 measured distributions. Training ranges of up to 20, 40, and approximately 76 MeV are compared with matched-size controls extending to 30 or 40 MeV while preserving the number of spectra per target. Above 40 MeV, the BMDN width mean absolute error relative to experiment decreases from 5.63 MeV for training up to 20 MeV to 2.70 and 2.53 MeV for two matched-size 30-MeV controls and 1.77 MeV for the matched-size 40-MeV control. Predictive-interval coverage relative to GEF above 40 MeV rises from 52.3% to 92.3% between the 20-MeV and full training ranges. Agreement with measured mean TKE and widths is target dependent, with no uniform ordering among GEF, MDN, and BMDN. The results demonstrate the sensitivity of high-energy widths to training-energy coverage, with improvements already evident at 30 MeV. The comparatively slow variation of GEF widths at high energies limits the extent to which these improvements can be attributed to learning nontrivial energy dependence. Adequate training-energy coverage is critical for reproducing the calculated high-energy evolution, while agreement with experiment remains constrained by the underlying fission model.
